### Task F6 — #7 is reproduced with the whole run's output kept, because the losing assertion is the deliverable and not the rate

**Depends on:** nothing   **Files:** none in the repository — this task writes run logs to `/var/tmp/m10-f7/` and a finding into the plan and into `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md`; it may **not** edit `tests/integration/test_rows_refresh.py`, which is F7's

**A flake reproduced without its traceback is a rate, not a mechanism**, and
that sentence is in issue #7 because the one reproduction M9 captured recorded
only `tail -2`. This task's exit condition is **the name of the assertion that
loses and the `E` line under it**, nothing else.

**The recorded rate has two denominators and they disagree about scope.**
`.claude/rules/mutation-sweeps.md`'s H7 entry says *"1 failure in 5 whole-suite
runs, 0 in 5 runs on its own"*; `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md:1033` says *"1 failure
in 5 whole-`tests/integration` runs, 0 in 5 runs on its own"* — the same
measurement written up twice with a different selection. The second
reproduction (H6's rework, *"once in two runs"*) takes the total to 2 in 7, and
the spec at `:177–181` and issue #7 both quote **2 in 7 whole-suite runs**,
which is a union of two differently-scoped denominators. Record which selection
each new run uses; the union is not a rate.

**Measured on this host 2026-08-13, before this plan was written: three whole
`tests/integration` runs under concurrent `tests/unit` load, and the target
moved.** `test_rows_refresh.py`'s three cases passed **3 of 3**. What failed was
`tests/integration/test_adapters_search_postgres.py` — three RRF-fusion cases
(`test_a_single_lane_row_does_not_outrank_the_row_both_lanes_found`,
`test_a_row_only_one_lane_found_is_still_returned`,
`test_a_title_deep_in_both_lanes_still_reaches_the_first_page`) failing together
in **1 of 3** runs, the second on *"fusion returned only what both lanes agreed
on; that is an INNER JOIN where a FULL OUTER JOIN belongs"* with two of the four
seeded titles absent from the fused set:

| run | load profile | result |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 3 × `tests/unit`, which finished after ~60 s | **3 failed**, 1,229 passed, 22 skipped, 173 s — all three failures inside the loaded window |
| 2 | 3 × `tests/unit` looping for the whole run | green |
| 3 | 3 × `tests/unit` looping for the whole run | green |

**So sustained load did not reproduce and a load *burst* coincided with three
failures, which is a correlation and not a mechanism** — the third time this
project has had to say that about a flake. **This tree has at least two
load-sensitive integration cases and only one of them is recorded anywhere.**
The debt entry's own rule arrives with the sign flipped: an inherited
*diagnosis* is a diagnosis nobody re-measured.

🔴 **The `-q`/`-qq` trap fired on the harness that produced this table.**
`pyproject.toml`'s `addopts` already carries `-q`, so runs 2 and 3 were invoked
`-qq` and printed **no `N passed` summary line at all** on a green run; the
verdict had to be recovered by counting `F` and `E` characters in the progress
output. The rule is in `.claude/rules/mutation-sweeps.md` and it is stated about
harnesses; it is restated here because this is the fourth recorded instance and
the second at an interactive shell. **Pass no verbosity flag.**

**How to run it, and the two things that make a run count.** Whole
`tests/integration`, output redirected to a file with `--tb=long -rA` and **no
`-q`** (`addopts` already carries one, so a second makes it `-qq` and suppresses
the summary line entirely — this repository has lost eight verdicts to that
trap). Load supplied by concurrent `tests/unit` runs kept alive for the *whole*
integration run: in the run above the load processes finished after ~60 s and
all three failures land inside that window, which is a correlation worth
testing rather than a mechanism. `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` and
`-p no:cacheprovider` throughout, so nothing is written into the tree. Ten
runs, or until the case fails twice, whichever comes first; each run's log kept
under `/var/tmp/m10-f7/` (**not** `/tmp`, which is tmpfs here) with a `sha256`
per file.

**The three claims, enumerated explicitly because the debt entry and H7's
write-up disagree about how many are orderings.** The case is
`test_rows_refresh.py:292–353`, its docstring names the three at `:302–316`, and
the assertions are:

1. **The response is the stale screen.** `assert [row["slug"] for row in
   response.json()["rows"]] == ["planted-stale"]` (`:328`). *Not* an ordering
   claim — no clock decides it; PRD 09's entry corrects H7's write-up on exactly
   this point.
2. **The request did not wait for the refresh.** `assert
   app.state.row_refreshes.depth == 1` (`:329`) and `assert
   app.state.row_refreshes.pending == frozenset({household})` (`:332`). An
   ordering claim, and the one the lane being stopped across the request
   (`await app.state.lanes.stop()`, `:319`, with `assert
   app.state.lanes.rows_refreshing() is False` at `:320` as its premise) makes a
   fact.
3. **The refresh opened a session of its own, after the request's had committed
   and closed.** Four assertions: `assert request_sessions` (`:335`), `assert
   request_sessions <= session_log.commits` (`:336`), `assert
   refresh_sessions.isdisjoint(request_sessions)` (`:344`) and `assert
   session_log.last_end(request_sessions) < session_log.first_begin(refresh_sessions)`
   (`:348`). An ordering claim **and** an identity claim, and the identity half
   is where F7 says the defect is. Note that only two of the seven assertions in
   the case carry a message, so a `tail -2` capture of a failure in this claim
   is very likely to show a bare `assert` line and nothing that names it — which
   is the second reason the whole traceback is the deliverable.

**Failing test first:** none, and that is the task rather than an exemption.
This is an observation, and a case written before the observation would be a
case written against a guess — F7's whole risk is a repair credited to a
scheduling change. What stands in its place is the **pre-registered run
protocol**: the selection, the load profile, the redirection, the run count and
the stopping rule are written to `/var/tmp/m10-f7/PROTOCOL.md` with a `sha256`
**before the first run**, so a protocol edited after a number was seen shows up
in the log. That is the same bar every measurement task in this project takes,
and `/var/tmp` rather than `/tmp` because `/tmp` is tmpfs on this host and a
bar whose whole value is that it provably predates the numbers cannot live in
RAM.

**Acceptance:**
- A run log exists at `/var/tmp/m10-f7/` in which
  `test_the_route_serves_stale_and_the_refresh_runs_on_a_session_of_its_own`
  failed, with the full `--tb=long` traceback captured, and the plan records
  **which of the seven assertions above fired and what its `E` line said**.
- The observed rate is reported with its selection named (`tests/integration`
  or the whole suite) and the load named (what else was running), because the
  existing 2-in-7 is a union of two different denominators.
- The three `test_adapters_search_postgres.py` failures get their **own**
  carried-debt entry in `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md`, with the 1-of-3 measurement,
  the traceback above and the load profile — **a second intermittent case found
  while chasing the first is not a footnote to the first**, and CI
  (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:46` — `uv run pytest --cov=usher
  --cov-report=term-missing`, whole suite, no deselection) is exactly as blind to
  it. Whether it is the same mechanism as #7 is an open question and is written
  down as one; the two share a host and a load profile and nothing else so far.
- **Nothing is deselected.** Not in CI, not in this task. The debt entry's
  warning stands: a case deselected in CI is a feature nobody checks, and the
  first red would then arrive with nothing anywhere saying it is known.
- If ten runs produce no failure, that is the result and it is reported as one —
  with the ten logs, the load profile and the host's load average per run — and
  F7 is **not** attempted on a hypothesis. A repair for an assertion nobody
  watched fail is a repair nobody can verify.

**Risks:**
- **The reproduction is allocation-sensitive and this host is not the CI
  runner.** 16 threads, load average ~5 at the time of the run above; a GitHub
  `ubuntu-latest` runner is 4 vCPU with a different allocator profile. A failure
  to reproduce here does not clear CI, which is why the acceptance criterion
  above is *"report the null result"* rather than *"close the issue"*.

### Task F7 — claim 3's premise is `id(session)`, and `id()` is reused by the next object in the same slot

**Depends on:** F6 (which assertion loses is genuinely unknown until it has been watched)   **Files:** `tests/integration/test_rows_refresh.py:206–248` (`_SessionLog` and its fixture) and `:292–353` (the case), `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md` (**only** the carried-debt bullet at `:1031–1072`), closes #7

**The carried-debt entry's prescription is already implemented, and this is the
correction that reframes the task.** `09-roadmap.md:1067–1072` says *"The honest
fix is to make the two ordering premises facts rather than races … observed
through a session log rather than forced."* `_SessionLog`
(`test_rows_refresh.py:189–214`) has existed since the file landed in
`7393c37` on **2026-08-11**, a day before H7 measured the flake on the merged
tree. **The session log is not the fix; it is the thing that is flaking.** The
spec inherits the prescription at `:177–181` and so does this group's brief.

**The mechanism, measured rather than reasoned about.** `_SessionLog` keys every
record on `id(session)` (`:235`, `:238`, `:241`) and stores nothing but that
integer. `id()` in CPython is the object's address and is reused the moment the
object is freed. Measured with `uv run` on this host, 2026-08-13:

```
2000 sequentially created-and-freed sqlalchemy.orm.Session objects
occupied 7 distinct id() values.
```

A bare `Session` is refcount-freed — a `weakref` to one dies on `del` with the
cycle collector disabled — and the next `Session()` takes the same slot. The
request's `AsyncSession` is opened and closed by `get_session`'s `async with`
and is unreferenced by the time the refresh lane runs, so a refresh session
landing in a freed request-session slot makes `refresh_sessions.isdisjoint(
request_sessions)` false — and that assertion's message is *"the refresh reused
the request's session — the `AsyncSession` hazard that usually works, which is
how it ships"*. **The worst available failure mode: a flake that accuses the
code of the exact defect the feature exists to prevent.** The same collision
also poisons `last_end(request_sessions)`, since the reused id then carries the
refresh's own `after_transaction_end` records, which is how the second ordering
assertion at `:347` can fire without any clock having misbehaved.

**Why it is intermittent rather than constant:** whether the refresh's `Session`
lands in *that* freed slot depends on what else pymalloc handed out of the same
size class between the two, and a whole-suite run's allocation churn is nothing
like a solo run's. That is a better fit for *0 in 5 solo, 2 in 7 under load*
than any clock explanation, and it explains why the case's own docstring —
*"Distinct `Session` identities"* — is describing a property the code does not
have.

**The repair, and it is three lines.** `_SessionLog` keeps a strong reference to
every session it records (`self._pinned: list[Session]`, appended in the
`after_begin` handler). A pinned object cannot be freed, so its `id()` cannot be
reused, so identity is sound for the log's lifetime. The premise that the
pinning worked is itself asserted — `len({id(s) for s in log.pinned}) ==
len(log.pinned)` — because a pinning that silently did not happen looks exactly
like one that did. The **time-based** classification stays and stays justified
by the stopped lane, which `_SessionLog`'s own docstring documents at
`:207–214`; what changes is that the sets it produces are sets of distinct
sessions.

**This repair is pre-registered, not assumed.** If F6's captured traceback names
a different assertion, F7 repairs *that* one and this section becomes a
recorded, refuted hypothesis in the plan rather than a fix nobody needed. Two
sentences in one document predicting opposite outcomes is how A1's sweep earned
its keep.

**Failing test first:** the case itself, run under the load profile F6
established, with the pinning **reverted** — i.e. F7's proof is that the repair
turns a reproducible red into a green under the identical load. Where a
mechanical red is wanted first, it is
`tests/integration/test_rows_refresh.py::test_the_session_log_identifies_sessions_by_object_and_not_by_a_reusable_address`
— constructs two `Session`s such that the second provably occupies the first's
freed address (the 2000-in-7 measurement above is the recipe), feeds both to a
`_SessionLog`, and asserts the log distinguishes them. At HEAD it fails, because
the log holds one integer for two sessions. Its positive control is the
assertion that the two addresses really did collide (`assert id(second) ==
first_id, "the allocator did not reuse the slot; this case proves nothing"`) —
without it, an allocator that handed out a fresh address passes the case for
the wrong reason, which is this repository's most-repeated failure shape.

**Acceptance:**
- `_SessionLog` identifies sessions by a reference it holds, not by an address
  it recorded, and the pinning premise is asserted in the fixture.
- The case's docstring names the identity hazard and the measurement (2,000
  `Session` objects, 7 distinct `id()` values, this host, this date), so the
  next reader does not "simplify" the pinning away as a leak.
- Under F6's load profile the case passes **N of N** runs where N is at least
  the number of runs F6 needed to see one failure, and the pre-repair red is
  reproduced at least once on the same profile — a repair whose before-state was
  never observed red is a repair credited to a scheduling change.
- `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md:1031–1072` is rewritten rather than struck: the
  prescription in it (*"observed through a session log"*) is corrected to say
  the log existed and was the defect, because that sentence has now been copied
  forward twice (into the M10 spec and into this group's brief) and will be
  copied again.
- **Still nothing is deselected in CI.**
- Sweep targets, expected verdict written first: the pinning list deleted must
  fail the new mechanical case; `commits` left keyed on `id()` while `begins`
  and `ends` are pinned must fail the `request_sessions <= commits` arm under a
  planted collision — the half-repair is the plausible one and it needs its own
  plant. Equivalent-mutant control: the two independent `event.listen` calls for
  `after_begin` and `after_commit` swapped in the fixture (`:243` and `:245`) —
  a fact about the code, since SQLAlchemy dispatches by event name and neither
  handler reads the other's state — measured against all five gate steps.

**Risks:**
- **The pinned sessions are held for the test's duration**, which is a
  deliberate leak and is bounded at a handful of objects per case. Named in the
  docstring so it is not read as an oversight.
- **A green after the repair is not proof the diagnosis was right** unless the
  red was observed on the same profile first. That is why the before/after pair
  is an acceptance criterion and not a report line.

### Task F8 — #10's scoped decision, and the five counts it rests on are an M8-head measurement M9 moved

**Depends on:** nothing   **Files:** a new ADR under `docs/prd/decisions/` and `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md` (**only** the carried-debt bullet at `:974–1004`). **No `src/` change** — this task produces the decision an implementer can be handed, which is what M9's boundary call 8 said was missing

**M9 declined this as its boundary call 8 and the reason is intact**: 31 of the
45 are written through `stage_records`' `copy_records_to_table` on the raw
asyncpg connection, where an out-of-range `int` raises a bare `OverflowError`
with no SQLSTATE, *"so no widening of `except IntegrityError` reaches them and
there is nothing to map."* Every re-measurement below leaves that argument
standing. What it does not leave standing is the arithmetic.

**Re-counted at HEAD `3adb5b0` on 2026-08-13, from the SQLAlchemy metadata and
from the migration files independently (they agree column for column; offline
`alembic upgrade head --sql` is unusable, dying at
`e5b8f2c40d17_ingest_pipeline.py:107` on `MockConnection`):**

| claim | verdict |
|---|---|
| **67 bounded columns** | **Correct at M8's head and stale by 8 today.** 22 `varchar(N)` + 44 `integer` + 1 `numeric(12,8)` = 67 at `m08b`. At `m09f` it is **75** (26 + 48 + 1), or 76 counting the one `bigint`, or **81** if CHECK-only value bounds count. M9 added `images.kind/width/height`, `search_queries.mode/result_count/latency_ms`, `title_search_names.kind` (`m09a`) and `credits.source` (`m09d`). |
| **17 provably safe** | **Unverifiable from source — no per-column ledger exists anywhere in the repository.** Applying `_errors.py:53–57`'s own rule ("the field is bounded on fewer sides than the column"), **16** are defensible at M8's head: 14 enum-backed `varchar` columns plus `jobs.priority` (`Field(ge=0, le=100)`, `domain/jobs.py:326`, mirrored by `ck_jobs_priority_range`) plus `genome_tags.tag_id` (bounded at the *batch* by `replace_genome_tags`, `bulk.py:446`). The 17th cannot be identified. Today the enum-backed set is 18. |
| **5 already translated** | **Confirmed, and exact.** `curated_rows.position` (`curation.py:227`) and `llm_calls.tokens_in`/`tokens_out`/`cost_usd`/`latency_ms` (`llm_call.py:109`). `llm_calls.purpose` is enum-backed and belongs in the safe bucket. **Stale**: five further tables have since taken `refusals_as_conflict` — `search_query.py:124,137`, `image.py:226`, `bulk.py:483,768`, `watch_state.py:468` — so the translated set is ~15 columns at HEAD, over 7 sites. |
| **45 exposed** | **Arithmetically consistent (67 − 17 − 5) and not independently reproducible.** With only 16 of the 17 pinnable the independent figure at M8's head is **46 ± 1**; at HEAD it is materially smaller, ~39, because M9 both added columns and translated five more tables. |
| **31 through the COPY path** | **Refuted as stated, by one, and the discrepancy is internal to the claim.** 37 narrow-staged bounded destination columns minus 5 provably safe among them = 31 **only if `media_items.file_size_bytes` (`bigint`) counts as bounded** — which is exactly the column the 67 excludes. Under one rule the pair is 68/31 or 67/30; it cannot be 67/31. |

**Two findings the claim does not contain, and both widen it.** First, the
staging tables are **not** wide: `staging.py:22–33` says they *"deliberately
carry no constraints"*, which is true of CHECKs and false of column types.
**Of 16 staging tables, exactly three declare a column wider than its
destination** — `stg_jobs.kind text` (`jobs.py:110`), `stg_titles.imdb_id text`
(`bulk.py:529`) and `stg_crosswalk.imdb_id text` (`bulk.py:901`) — which are the
three that behave like the roadmap's own `id_crosswalk.imdb_id` worked example.
Everything else mirrors the destination's width, including
`stg_media_items.container varchar(32)`, which is the roadmap's counter-example.
Second, **six `bulk.py` methods have no `except` at all** — `upsert_titles`
(`:523`), `apply_ratings` (`:621`), `fill_credit_names` (`:649`),
`upsert_tmdb_ids` (`:866`), `upsert_crosswalk` (`:896`),
`upsert_genome_vectors` (`:391`) — so those leak raw at the `INSERT … SELECT`
stage too, which is a second leak on a path the candidate fix is supposed to
route refusals *onto*.

**And there are two failure shapes on the COPY path, not one.** An out-of-range
`int` into a staged `integer` is refused **client-side** by asyncpg's binary
encoder as `builtins.OverflowError` — no SQLSTATE, not a `DBAPIError`,
`is_row_refusal()` cannot inspect it. An over-length string into a staged
`varchar(32)` is refused **server-side** during the COPY, as an
`asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError` carrying `22001` — a real
SQLSTATE on an exception that is still not a `DBAPIError`, because it is raised
on the raw driver connection outside SQLAlchemy's translation. Issue #10 names
only the first. **A decision that widens `bigint` and forgets `text` fixes one
of the two.**

**What the decision has to settle, and none of it is a patch.** (1) Which rule
defines a bounded column, stated once, so the total and the COPY figure cannot
disagree again — `bigint` in or out, CHECK-only bounds in or out. (2) Whether
widening the 16 staging DDLs to `bigint`/`text` is the fix, given that it moves
refusal to the `INSERT … SELECT` where six of the writers have no `except`
either — so the candidate is **two** changes, not one. (3) What the destination
statement should then answer: `refusals_as_conflict` at every COPY writer, or
`except IntegrityError` widened per site, and `_errors.py:66–75`'s narrowness
caveat (class 22 is only "about the row" for a parameterised statement with no
server-side expression, which an `INSERT … SELECT` with a `CAST` is not) is the
argument that has to be made rather than assumed. (4) Whether `stg_genome.tmdb_id
integer` (`bulk.py:221`) — a staging-only join key with **no destination column
at all**, so it is in none of the 67 — is in scope; a MovieLens `tmdb_id` above
2³¹ raises `OverflowError` in the COPY and corresponds to nothing anybody has
counted. `stg_credit_names.ordinal` and `stg_akas.ordering` are the same shape.
(5) Whether the domain models are bounded instead of, or as well as, the
columns — see F9.

**Failing test first:** none, and that is the task. A design task whose output
is an ADR has no red to write; what it has instead is a **falsifiable
enumeration**, and the enumeration is the deliverable that F9 turns into a test.
The one mechanical artefact F8 does produce is the script that generates the
per-column ledger — `scripts/` or a throwaway, its choice — because *"17
provably safe"* has been quoted three times in two milestones and cannot be
reproduced from anything in this repository.

**Acceptance:**
- A new ADR states the bounding rule, publishes the **per-column ledger** the
  three quoted counts have never had, and answers (1)–(5) above with reasons.
- Every number in `09-roadmap.md:974–1004` is corrected in place with its
  measurement date and its revision, and the corrections say which were right at
  the time — 67 and 5 were, 31 was not under the rule that produced 67, and 17
  cannot be reproduced at all.
- The ADR names its own scope boundary: which of the exposed columns M10 fixes
  and which it does not, so F9 is a bounded task and not "translate the schema".
- The ADR does **not** re-open M9's boundary call 8. Its reason — 31 of the
  writes never reach a SQLAlchemy `except` — survives every re-measurement here
  and is restated with the two failure shapes rather than the one.

### Task F9 — the decision is implemented, and `Title.popularity` accepts infinity into a `double precision` column, not the `NUMERIC` the record names

**Depends on:** F8   **Files:** as F8's ADR scopes them, plus `src/usher/domain/title.py:57–59`, `tests/unit/test_domain_title.py`, `tests/integration/test_bulk_repository.py`, `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md` (**only** the same bullet F8 corrects)

**The infinity half is confirmed by running it, and the record is wrong about
the column type.** `domain/title.py:59` is `popularity: float | None =
Field(default=None, ge=0)`, and measured on this host 2026-08-13:
`float('inf') >= 0` is `True`, `json.loads('1e400')` is `inf`, and
`Title(..., popularity=json.loads('1e400')).popularity` is `inf` — reachable
straight off a TMDb payload. `community_rating` (`:57`) is *"safe only by
accident of its `le=10`"*, confirmed: `le=10` refuses both `inf` and `NaN`, and
`ge=0` alone refuses `-inf` and `NaN` but not `+inf`. **But
`09-roadmap.md:991–992`'s *"Postgres 17's unbounded `NUMERIC` stores it"* names
the wrong type**: `titles.popularity` is `sa.Float()` → `double precision`
(`db/models/title.py:115`, `a8a0e10ff464_core_schema.py:102`), as is
`tmdb_ids.popularity` (`db/models/bootstrap.py:97`). IEEE `Infinity` is a legal
`float8` and satisfies the CHECK `popularity IS NULL OR popularity >= 0`
(`a8a0e10ff464_core_schema.py:142`). The behaviour is exactly as claimed and the
mechanism is not the one recorded — which matters, because a reader who goes
looking for a `NUMERIC` precision to widen will not find one.

**Two more the record does not name, found by running the same probe:**
`Title(..., year=2**31)` and `Title(..., vote_count=2**31)` both construct
cleanly. `titles.year` and `titles.vote_count` are `integer`. So the
`Field(ge=0)`-against-`integer` shape `db-and-sql.md` names as *"the common
shape here"* is live on the two columns a bulk import writes most of.

**The fix's shape is F8's to decide and its verification is this task's.**
Whatever F8 rules, the implementation is bounded by one property that a test can
state without knowing the answer: **a value a domain model accepts must not
reach an operator as a raw driver exception.** That is `ADR-0009`'s rule and it
is what `_errors.py` exists for.

**Failing test first:**
`tests/integration/test_bulk_repository.py::test_a_value_the_domain_model_accepts_is_refused_as_a_port_error_and_never_as_an_encoder_crash`
— parametrised over the ledger F8 publishes, drives each column's real
repository method with the smallest value the domain model accepts and the
column cannot hold, and asserts `pytest.raises(UsherPortError)`. At HEAD the
`integer`-staged arms fail with `builtins.OverflowError` and the
`varchar(N)`-staged arms with a bare
`asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError`, neither of which is a
`UsherPortError`. Its positive control is that at least one arm **passes** at
HEAD — the five already-translated columns — so a parametrisation that
collected nothing, or one whose values were all in range, cannot read as
coverage; a scan that globs nothing passes exactly like a scan that passes.

**Acceptance:**
- Every column F8 scoped answers a `UsherPortError` for a value its domain model
  accepts, driven **through the real repository method** and against real
  Postgres, never through a hand-written statement.
- `Title.popularity` refuses a non-finite value, or the column refuses it, and
  the choice is F8's; whichever it is, `json.loads('1e400')` through the shipped
  TMDb mapper is a case. `year` and `vote_count` get the same treatment or an
  explicit, reasoned exclusion.
- `09-roadmap.md`'s *"Postgres 17's unbounded `NUMERIC`"* is corrected to
  `double precision` with the CHECK it satisfies, because a wrong mechanism in a
  debt entry sends the next reader to the wrong file.
- If the staging DDLs are widened, the **six `bulk.py` writers with no `except`
  at all** gain one in the same commit — otherwise the fix moves a raw
  `OverflowError` into a raw `DBAPIError` and calls it translated.
- Sweep targets, expected verdict written first: each widened staging column
  narrowed back must fail its own parametrised arm and nothing else — a plant
  whose blast radius is much wider than the ledger predicted is a mis-spelling
  until proved otherwise; a widened `except` narrowed to `IntegrityError` must
  fail the arms whose SQLSTATE is class 22 and **not** those whose refusal is
  a constraint, which is the distinction `_errors.py:76` encodes. Any plant that
  changes an `except` clause is checked for the names it introduces before its
  verdict is believed — a `NameError` on the failure path produces a plausible
  kill naming exactly the cases the mutation was aimed at.

**Risks:**
- **Widening a staging column changes what `COPY` accepts and therefore what a
  malformed dump can push into a batch.** The refusal moves from row-time to
  statement-time, so one bad row now fails the whole `INSERT … SELECT` rather
  than the whole `COPY` — the same blast radius, one statement later, and worth
  saying rather than discovering.
- `stg_titles.imdb_id` and `stg_crosswalk.imdb_id` are already `text` against a
  `varchar(16)` destination, so **the candidate fix's own worked example is
  already in force on two of the three columns it cites**, and neither has a
  translated writer. That is evidence the widening alone is insufficient, and it
  is available before any code is written.

---

### Task F10 — #8's cause, and the reason no stack was recorded is Usher's own error boundary rather than a missing flag

**Depends on:** Phase 0's observability stack (the instrument), not on any task in this group   **Files:** `src/usher/cli.py:441–541` (`_work`'s loop), `src/usher/api/lanes.py:583–594`, `tests/unit/test_cli_errors.py`, and a finding written into `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md`'s carried-debt section

**The exit condition is a cause, not a fix.** M9's S3 ran 1.98 h of live TMDb
traffic; one of three workers died at 78 minutes on
`MissingGreenlet: greenlet_spawn has not been called; can't call await_only()
here`, after ~92,000 jobs, stranding 20 claims. W1's lease and `recover()`
(ADR-0037) removed the operational damage — **what remains is a crash that
self-heals** — so a task promising a fix would promise an unknown. Small fix
ships here; large fix becomes an issue with a *known* cause, which is strictly
better than today.

**The first finding is available now and it changes what "run it again" means.**
Issue #8 says *"the run used bare `usher work`, so no stack was recorded. Run
`usher --traceback work`."* That is a workaround for a structural asymmetry, not
an oversight. Verified: `MissingGreenlet` → `InvalidRequestError` →
**`SQLAlchemyError`**, and `SQLAlchemyError` is in `cli.OPERATOR_ERRORS`
(`cli.py:157`), so `issubclass(MissingGreenlet, cli.OPERATOR_ERRORS)` is `True`
— **`usher work` answers a `MissingGreenlet` with one sentence and exit 1, by
design of ADR-0026's boundary.** ADR-0026's own bar is *"a family belongs in the
tuple when an operator can act on it: start the database, fix the URL, reconnect
the network"*, and nothing an operator does fixes a `MissingGreenlet`. The
tuple's comment (`cli.py:155–157`) says `SQLAlchemyError` is there for *"a
missing table (`alembic upgrade head` never ran), a dead pool, a permission the
role does not have"* — all true, and all disjoint from
`InvalidRequestError`. **The boundary is too wide by exactly one subtree, and
that subtree is Usher's own bugs.** The other root loses the stack too, for a
different reason: `api/lanes.py:588–593` catches `Exception` and logs
`logger.warning("the worker lane's pass failed: {error}", error=str(exc))` — a
message, not `logger.exception`, so no frames. **Neither root records a
traceback for this class of failure**, which is why 92,000 jobs produced a rate
and no evidence.

**Second: `usher work` has no per-pass guard at all**, which the lane does.
`cli.py:530–535` is a bare loop over `_measure()`; `services/jobs.py:362–366`
re-raises the first task failure after every task has settled, so **one job's
exception ends the process**. The lane at `lanes.py:588` keeps going. That
asymmetry is worth naming on its own: the two roots have different survival
semantics for the same defect and no document says so.

**What the cause is not, established by reading rather than by re-running.**
`grep -c relationship src/usher/db/models/` is **0** across 29 mapped classes,
so a lazy *relationship* load — the canonical `MissingGreenlet` — is
unreachable. `db/base.py:121` builds every session factory with
`expire_on_commit=False`, so the commit-expiry family is unreachable. No mapped
class defines `__repr__`; no `logger.*` call in `src/` formats a mapped object
(every enrich-lane error line reads scalars off domain models —
`enrich.py:413–417`, `:437–442`); no ORM row escapes a repository. There is no
`TaskGroup`, no `run_sync` on the worker path, and no signal handler anywhere in
`src/usher`. And at HEAD the worker is a **bounded pool of scopes**, one
`AsyncSession` per job (`services/jobs.py:409`), so the shared-session theory
W1 already refuted is refuted a second way.

**The three candidates worth instrumenting, ranked, each with what refutes it.**

1. **A savepoint rollback expires the mapped row, and a later synchronous
   attribute read refreshes it.** `PostgresTitleRepository.update`
   (`title.py:346–389`) mutates a loaded `TitleRow` inside `begin_nested()` and
   flushes; on the `ix_titles_imdb_id` conflict the savepoint rolls back, and
   SQLAlchemy 2.0.51's `SessionTransaction._restore_snapshot(dirty_only=nested)`
   expires every modified state. `_to_domain` (`title.py:93–106`) is **31
   synchronous `getattr` calls on a mapped instance** — the only shape in this
   codebase that turns an expired attribute into I/O. It is armed by exactly the
   path the crash's last two log records name, 19 ms and 24 ms before death.
   **Refuted at HEAD for the cross-job case** — one session per job means the
   identity map lives ~0.5 s and holds one title — and *not* refuted for the
   within-job window: `services/enrich.py:186–193` catches the
   `RepositoryConflict` and calls `_record_failure` (`:423–442`), which issues a
   second `update()` on that expired row. That second call re-reads through an
   awaited `session.get`, so it is safe today and nothing tests that it stays
   safe.
2. **`defer(TitleRow.credit_names)` ships deliberately without `raiseload`**
   (`title.py:152–155`, argued at `:140–151`). It is the one attribute in the
   repository whose unloaded access degrades to a *silent lazy column load* —
   which under asyncio is not a degradation, it is `MissingGreenlet`. The
   comment chose it over `raiseload` to *"prefer the failure that degrades"*,
   which is exactly backwards on an async engine. Its sibling `search_document`
   carries `raiseload=True` and would raise `InvalidRequestError` under a
   different name; the crash was `MissingGreenlet`, which points here.
   **Narrowed**: no reader in `src/` reaches `credit_names` through a loaded
   `TitleRow` — the comment says the whole suite was run with `raiseload=True`
   to prove it — so the hazard is armed and currently unreached, by convention
   and not by a standing assertion.
3. **The raw asyncpg `COPY` inside a SQLAlchemy savepoint.**
   `db/staging.py:96–159` reaches through two wrappers to
   `driver_connection` and calls `copy_records_to_table` — the one place where
   I/O on the session's own connection bypasses SQLAlchemy's greenlet adapter
   entirely — and it runs on **every successful enrich job**
   (`enrich.py:270–277` → `jobs.py:303–322`), i.e. ~130,000 times in S3.
   **Refuted as a direct cause**: the `await` is legal, asyncpg is natively
   async, so this cannot itself raise `MissingGreenlet`. It survives as a
   state-corruption candidate, and a rate of one failure in 92,000 fits a
   rare-state bug rather than a per-call one.

**Phase 0's stack is the instrument, and this is what it is for.** A crash that
leaves a span and a `trace_id`-carrying log line is a different problem from one
that leaves nothing: with the OTLP endpoint populated, the `job.*` span for the
dying job carries the `ix_titles_imdb_id` conflict as a child and the correlated
log line names the job key, which turns "the last two records were conflicts" —
a correlation across ~30 non-fatal conflicts on the same process — into a
question about one job.

**Failing test first:**
`tests/unit/test_cli_errors.py::test_a_sqlalchemy_bug_in_ushers_own_code_keeps_its_traceback`
— raises `sqlalchemy.exc.MissingGreenlet` out of a dispatched command and
asserts the traceback survives, which is ADR-0026's own stated property
(*"a bug still reads like a bug"*, pinned today only by
`test_a_programming_error_keeps_its_traceback`, which uses an `AttributeError`).
At HEAD it fails: `SQLAlchemyError` catches it and `main` prints one line. Its
positive control is the sibling assertion that a genuinely operator-fixable
`SQLAlchemyError` — a missing table — still gets the sentence, because a repair
that narrowed the tuple to nothing would pass the first half.

**Acceptance:**
- `usher work` records a full traceback for a failure it cannot classify:
  `InvalidRequestError` (or a narrower spelling F10 argues for) leaves
  `OPERATOR_ERRORS`, and the loop at `cli.py:530–535` gains the per-pass
  `except` the lane has — with `logger.exception`, not `logger.warning`, at
  **both** roots, so `lanes.py:588–593` stops discarding frames too. **This is
  the deliverable even if the cause is never found**, and it is what makes the
  next occurrence evidence.
- ADR-0026 records the narrowing with the measurement
  (`issubclass(MissingGreenlet, cli.OPERATOR_ERRORS)` was `True`) and the rule it
  restates: the tuple names failures an operator can act on, and a SQLAlchemy
  *usage* error is not one.
- A reproduction attempt is made and reported either way: `usher work` at
  `USHER_JOB_CONCURRENCY` > 1 against a catalog seeded to force repeated
  `ix_titles_imdb_id` conflicts on the enrich lane, run long enough to pass
  92,000 jobs or bounded by a stated wall clock, with the traceback capture in
  place. **A null result is a result** and is recorded with its duration and its
  job count.
- Candidate 1's premise stops being an inference: a case asserts that a caught
  `IntegrityError` from `PostgresTitleRepository.update` leaves the row
  `inspect(row).expired is True`. That is checkable today, it is the one fact
  the whole candidate rests on, and it is currently derived from reading
  SQLAlchemy's source rather than from this repository.
- Candidate 2 is settled one way or the other: either `raiseload=True` on
  `credit_names` (which converts a process kill into a loud
  `InvalidRequestError`) or a standing structural assertion that no reader
  reaches it through a loaded `TitleRow` — the comment's *"verified rather than
  assumed"* is a claim about a run in 2026-08, not a guard.
- The outcome is one of two, written as such: **a cause with evidence**, in
  which case a small fix ships here and a large one becomes an issue naming it;
  or **the candidates above narrowed with what refuted each**, in which case #8
  stays open carrying a shorter list and the instrumentation that will catch the
  next one. Either closes the milestone's acceptance criterion, which asks for a
  *known cause* rather than a fix.

**Risks:**
- **The schedule is unknown by construction.** It is in Phase 2 rather than
  Phase 1 so it is on no critical path, and it must not acquire one: if the
  reproduction has not fired by the phase gate, the instrumentation half ships
  and the diagnosis half becomes the issue.
- **Narrowing `OPERATOR_ERRORS` makes some genuine operator failures louder.**
  `InvalidRequestError` also covers a few misuse cases an operator can
  provoke through configuration. The narrowing is measured per subclass against
  the fifteen commands, on ADR-0026's own bar, rather than applied whole — the
  same discipline the 2026-08-07 amendment used when it added three of
  `UsherPortError`'s nine subclasses and refused six.
# Group R — Phase 3, Release

Thirteen tasks. Phase 3 is the milestone's droppable phase
(`docs/specs/2026-08-13-m10-hardening-design.md:67`) and it is the only one whose
audience is a stranger, so every task here is judged by what somebody who has
never read `docs/` can do with the result.

**Four corrections to the spec, made here rather than left standing.** Each is
re-measured below in the task that owns it, with the command that produced it.

1. **`__version__` exists and has a live consumer.** The spec says
   `pyproject.toml:3`'s version is consumed by nothing — *"no `__version__`, no
   `--version`, nothing in the API surface"* (`:198–201`). One of those three is
   false: `src/usher/__init__.py:6` is `__version__ = version("usher")` behind a
   `PackageNotFoundError` fallback, and `src/usher/adapters/emby/session.py:166`
   takes it as `app_version`'s default, which reaches Emby on every request in
   the `MediaBrowser Client="Usher", …, Version="…"` identity header. So R1 is
   **not** wiring a version through; it is adding the two surfaces that are
   genuinely missing and pinning the agreement that already holds.
2. **The git-history finding is three files and 113 commits, not one file and two
   rows.** `docs/plans/2026-07-30-m2-bootstrap.md` carries the same rows
   *verbatim* across **113** of `origin/main`'s 678 commits — seventeen more than
   either fixture — and it was fixed by a **second** commit, `969dcbf`, eighteen
   minutes after `1196a9d`. R12 has the counts.
3. **`README:10` is `## Status`; the word "Pre-release." is at `README:12`.** And
   TMDb's logo sentence spans `README:696–698`, not `:697`.
4. **`08-operations.md:585–588` is right about the README instruction** and the
   sentence is at `:587` (*"State this loudly in the README"*). Verified.

**Files this group shares with others, declared so the orchestrator can
sequence rather than discover:** `README.md` (R2, R3, R9, R10, R11, R13 — six
tasks, disjoint anchors, and R13 rewrites the setup path R9–R11 add sections
beside), `pyproject.toml` (R1 only, and only if the version is bumped),
`src/usher/api/dto/health.py` + `src/usher/api/routers/health.py` (R2 only, and
Group F's failure-mode work touches `/health/ready` rather than `/health`),
`docs/prd/07-client-api.md` (R2's Meta row only), `docs/prd/04-catalog-bootstrap.md`
(R12's one paragraph, inserted between `:637` and `:639`),
`.github/workflows/` (R8 adds a file; nothing else in M10 touches CI),
`tests/unit/test_api_health.py`, `tests/unit/test_api_problem.py` and
`tests/integration/test_health.py` (R2 only — one exact-equality assertion each).

⚠️ **One README anchor is contested and the order is decided here rather than
discovered at a merge conflict.** R10 (the admin posture) and R13 (the
quickstart) both want the gap between `## Requirements` (ends `:112`) and
`## Running it` (`:113`). **R10's paragraph goes first**, inside `## Requirements`
as its last subsection, and R13's `## Quickstart` heading follows it — because
the posture is a precondition for running the thing and a quickstart that leads
a reader past it has defeated R10's whole reason for the placement. R13 rebases
over R10, not the other way round.

**R9 depends on Group K** (`usher backup` / `usher restore`) and is the only
task here that depends on another group at all. R13 depends on all twelve.

---

### Task R1 — `--version`, and the three sources that already agree

**Depends on:** nothing   **Files:** `src/usher/cli.py` (**only** `build_parser`, at `:1574–1587`), `tests/unit/test_cli.py`, `tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py` (new)

**The spec's premise is half wrong and the half that survives is the useful
half.** `pyproject.toml:3` reads `version = "0.1.0"`; `src/usher/__init__.py:3–10`
already reads it back with `importlib.metadata.version("usher")` under a
`PackageNotFoundError` fallback to `"0.0.0+unknown"`; and
`src/usher/adapters/emby/session.py:85,166` imports it as the default
`app_version`, which `_identity_header` (`:186–190`) interpolates into the
`MediaBrowser` header every Emby request carries. Measured 2026-08-13:
`uv run python -c "import usher, importlib.metadata as m; print(usher.__version__, m.version('usher'))"`
answers `0.1.0 0.1.0`. So the claim *"nothing consumes it"* is false — a third
party has been reading this number since M3.

**What is genuinely absent is `--version`** — `grep -rn '\-\-version' src/`
returns nothing — **and the API surface**, which is R2's.

**`importlib.metadata` is the right read and its one trap has to be stated.**
The build backend is `hatchling` (`pyproject.toml:70–72`) with the version
**static** in `[project]` — there is no `[tool.hatch.version]` table, no
`__about__.py`, and hatchling therefore writes no `__version__` anywhere; it
copies `[project].version` into the distribution's `METADATA`. `uv sync`
installs this project **editable** (`.venv/lib/python3.13/site-packages/usher-0.1.0.dist-info/direct_url.json`
is `{"dir_info":{"editable":true}}`, and the loader is `_editable_impl_usher.pth`),
so **the dist-info is a snapshot taken at install time and `pyproject.toml` is
not read at runtime.** Bumping `pyproject.toml:3` without re-running `uv sync`
leaves `usher.__version__` on the old number and a running process reporting it.
That is not a reason to read the TOML at runtime — a wheel has no
`pyproject.toml` in it — it is the reason the agreement test below is worth
having, and the reason an implementer who bumps the version must `uv sync`
before believing a green suite.

`build_parser` (`:1574`) already carries one top-level flag, `--traceback`
(`:1582–1586`), with the argument for top-level-ness written beside it. `--version`
goes there, spelled `action="version"`. **The placement is load-bearing rather
than stylistic:** `main` (`:1886–1887`) is
`argv = sys.argv[1:] if argv is None else list(argv)` then
`parse_args(list(argv) if argv else ["serve"])`, and only *after* that does the
`try` open and `get_settings()` run. So an `action="version"` flag raises
`SystemExit(0)` from inside `parse_args`, before any settings are read and before
any database is touched — which is the whole point: `docker run --rm
ghcr.io/anirudhlath/usher:v0.1.0 python -m usher --version` has to answer on a
host with no Postgres. `main`'s own docstring (`:1879–1884`) already records that
`SystemExit` passes through the boundary untouched because it is a
`BaseException` and the handlers name only `Exception` subclasses; this adds a
sixth site to the five it lists.

**Failing test first:** `tests/unit/test_cli.py::test_version_prints_the_package_version_and_reads_no_settings`
— drives `usher.cli.main(["--version"])` under `pytest.raises(SystemExit)`,
asserts `excinfo.value.code == 0` and that captured stdout is exactly
`f"usher {usher.__version__}\n"`, **and** that `get_settings` was never called
(monkeypatch it to raise). At HEAD argparse answers `error: unrecognized
arguments: --version` and exits **2**, so the case fails on the exit code before
it reaches the string. Its positive control is the second arm: the same test
asserts `usher.__version__` is truthy and is not the `"0.0.0+unknown"` fallback,
because an uninstalled tree makes both sides of the stdout comparison equal to
the fallback and the assertion vacuous.

**Second case, a guard rather than a discovery — say so in its docstring:**
`tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py::test_the_three_version_sources_agree`
reads `[project].version` out of `pyproject.toml` with `tomllib`,
`importlib.metadata.version("usher")`, and `usher.__version__`, and asserts all
three are equal. **Verified true at HEAD before it is written.** Its control is
the same non-fallback assertion. Its docstring records what a red means: not a
bug in the code, but a `pyproject.toml` edited without a `uv sync`.

**Acceptance:**
- `uv run usher --version` prints `usher 0.1.0` and exits 0; `uv run usher
  --version` with `USHER_DATABASE_URL` unset and `USHER_SECRET_KEY` unset does
  the same, which is the property `--traceback` does not have and which the test
  pins by monkeypatch rather than by environment.
- `uv run usher` with no arguments still starts the HTTP server. `main`'s
  `argv or ["serve"]` is untouched, and `tests/unit/test_main.py`'s two cases —
  which exist because `argv is None` once made `usher sync-status` silently start
  the server — pass unchanged.
- `tests/unit/test_cli_errors.py::test_every_command_reports_a_dead_database_the_same_way`
  is unchanged and still green. `--version` is not a subcommand, so it does not
  join `_dispatch`'s table and does not owe that table's case — stated because
  `.claude/rules/config-cli-and-deployment.md` records that *"a new command owes
  this case; the boundary table does not supply it"*, and this is deliberately
  not a new command.
- `src/usher/__init__.py` is **not** edited. The fallback string, the
  `# pragma: no cover` and the `__all__` all stay as they are.
- Sweep targets, expected verdict written first: `action="version"` respelled as
  `action="store_true"` with a print in `_dispatch` must fail the
  reads-no-settings arm (it now runs after `get_settings()`); `version="usher
  %(prog)s"` in place of the interpolated `__version__` must fail the stdout
  equality; and the equivalent-mutant control is **swapping the written order of
  `--traceback` and `--version` in `build_parser`** — a fact about the code, since
  argparse stores optionals in a dict keyed by `dest` and nothing in this
  repository reads `parser._actions` — which must pass all five gate steps.

### Task R2 — `/health` names the version, and the argument against publishing it is answered rather than skipped

**Depends on:** R1   **Files:** `src/usher/api/dto/health.py` (`LivenessResponse`, `:13–14`), `src/usher/api/routers/health.py` (`:41–44`), `tests/unit/test_api_health.py` (`:71`), `tests/unit/test_api_problem.py` (`:470`), `tests/integration/test_health.py` (`:55`), `docs/prd/07-client-api.md` (**only** the Meta table row at `:918`)

`LivenessResponse` is two lines and one field (`api/dto/health.py:13–14`), and
`/health` answers `LivenessResponse(status="ok")` (`routers/health.py:44`). It
gains a `version: str`, filled from `usher.__version__`. **`/health` and not
`/health/ready`**, for the reason `ReadinessChecks`' own docstring gives
(`dto/health.py:19–27`): every field on that model becomes part of the status
code via `all(checks.model_dump().values())`, and a version string is not a
check. It also keeps this task off the model V1 planted against
(`.claude/rules/mutation-sweeps.md`, plant `b2` — `ReadinessResponse` growing
`type`/`code` fails `assert "type" not in body`).

**The objection, stated because a stranger will make it.** This box is
internet-facing behind `nginx-proxy-manager`, `/health` is unauthenticated, and
a version string on an unauthenticated probe is a free answer to *"which CVEs
apply"*. Three things answer it and none of them is "nobody will look". The
repository is public and MIT (`gh repo view` 2026-08-13: `visibility: PUBLIC`),
so the tag, the changelog and the lockfile are already readable by the same
person. `/openapi.json` is served unauthenticated and describes the whole
surface (`README:81`), which is a strictly larger disclosure. And the thing it
buys is the one cheap way an operator can tell which image is actually running,
which is exactly what an incident needs. **Published, and the operator who
disagrees can drop `/health` at the reverse proxy** — one location block, and it
costs them the compose healthcheck, which targets `/health/ready`
(`compose.yml`'s `test:` line) and is unaffected. Write that trade into
`LivenessResponse`'s docstring, not only into this plan.

**PRD 07 is additive here and says so itself.** *"DTOs are versioned
independently … Additive changes ship freely; breaking changes get `/v2`"*
(`07-client-api.md:958–962`). The Meta table row at `:918` gains the field name;
nothing else in that document moves.

**Failing test first:** `tests/unit/test_api_health.py::test_liveness_names_the_running_version`
— `GET /health` against `create_app()` and asserts
`response.json() == {"status": "ok", "version": usher.__version__}`. At HEAD the
body is `{"status": "ok"}` and the equality fails on the missing key. Its
positive control is that the asserted version is not `"0.0.0+unknown"` — an
uninstalled tree would otherwise make the case pass against a `/health` that
reports nothing useful. The equality is deliberate rather than
`"version" in body`: an absence assertion cannot tell a served version from a
served empty string.

**Three existing exact-equality assertions go red and all three are updated,
not loosened:** `tests/unit/test_api_health.py:71`,
`tests/unit/test_api_problem.py:470` and `tests/integration/test_health.py:55`
each read `assert response.json() == {"status": "ok"}`. They are equalities on
purpose — `test_api_health.py:62–71`'s case is the liveness/readiness split's own
proof — so each becomes the two-key equality. **Do not weaken any of them to a
subset check**; the third is in the integration file and is the only one that
runs against a real Postgres.

**Acceptance:**
- `curl -sf http://localhost:8100/health` answers `{"status":"ok","version":"0.1.0"}`
  against the compose stack, and the container healthcheck (`/health/ready`,
  `compose.yml`) is unchanged and still passes.
- `/openapi.json`'s `LivenessResponse` schema declares both fields as required.
  `tests/unit/test_api_openapi.py`'s conformance scan is unaffected — this adds
  no route and no non-2xx response, so neither of H2's two directions moves.
- `tests/unit/test_api_dto.py`'s three scans still pass: `version` is not in
  `_FORBIDDEN_NAMES`, is not `SecretStr`, and `LivenessResponse` remains in the
  discovery walk's named anchor set (`:54`).
- `README:121`'s `curl -sf http://localhost:8100/health  # {"status":"ok"}`
  comment is corrected in the same commit. It is one line and it is the first
  thing in the README that would become false.
- Sweep targets, expected verdict written first: `version=""` in the constructor
  must fail the new case; `version=usher.__version__` moved onto
  `ReadinessResponse` instead must fail it too *and* leave `/health/ready`'s own
  cases green, which is the measurement that says the field landed on the right
  model; the control is **swapping the two field declarations in
  `LivenessResponse`** — a fact about the code, since every construction in
  `src/` and `tests/` binds by keyword and pydantic serialises by declaration
  order into a dict every case compares as a mapping — passing all five gate
  steps.

### Task R3 — the release is `v0.1.0`, and the reason is written where somebody choosing a version number will trip over it

**Depends on:** R1   **Files:** `docs/prd/decisions/00NN-the-release-is-v0-1-0.md` (new — see the numbering note), `README.md` (a new `## Versioning` section between `## Attribution` at `:674` and `## License` at `:712`), `README.md:12`, `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md` (**only** the `## v1 — the abstraction works end to end` heading's paragraph at `:14–17`)

**The number is contested, which is the bar `prd-maintenance.md` sets for an
ADR** (*"write one only when the call was contested — where a reasonable person
would choose differently"*). `09-roadmap.md:14` heads the M1–M10 table *"v1 —
the abstraction works end to end"* and `:16–17` gives the success condition — *"a
client can be built against Usher that fully replaces direct Emby access"* —
which M9 met. A reasonable reader gets from there to `1.0.0` in one step, and the
step is wrong: **"v1" there names a scope milestone and "1.0.0" in semver names a
compatibility promise.**

The compatibility promise is not true, and the evidence is countable rather than
felt. There is **no authentication anywhere** — `grep -rn
"fastapi.security\|HTTPBearer\|APIKeyHeader\|OAuth2\|HTTPBasic" src/ tests/`
returns exactly one hit and it is prose in `adapters/emby/session.py:15` — and
it is a *Post-v1 candidate* (`09-roadmap.md:1338–1339`), not a deferral with a
date. There is **one source adapter**: `usher.adapters.emby` is the only member of
the `forbidden_modules` list at `pyproject.toml:228`, and PRD 09 names Jellyfin
and Plex as *"the genuine test of the abstraction"* (`:1340–1342`) — an
abstraction whose test has not been run. And **seven feature issues stay open**
after M10 closes the ten it takes: #11, #12, #14, #15, #16, #18, #21
(`gh issue list --state open`, 2026-08-13, seventeen open of which M10 closes
ten). #21 is a ranking change, #11 adds two bulk parsers and a writer, #18 adds
an auth mode to `POST /admin/sources`. Every one of them moves a wire contract.

`0.x` says all of that in three characters. `1.0.0` remains available the day
it is meant.

**Where it goes, and the placement is the task.** An ADR alone is a decision
nobody meets. Three anchors:

- **The ADR** carries the argument, in `decisions/`'s context → decision →
  consequences → evidence shape, with the three counts above as its evidence.
  ⚠️ **Numbering:** the highest ADR at HEAD is
  `0038-the-embedding-width-is-deployment-wide-ddl.md`, so `0039` is next — and
  Phases 0–2 may take it. Run `ls docs/prd/decisions/` at the moment the task
  lands rather than trusting this line.
- **`README:12`'s "Pre-release." becomes "Beta."**, followed by one sentence
  naming the policy and linking the ADR. That word is the first content a
  stranger reads and it currently promises less than the project delivers.
- **`09-roadmap.md:14–17`** gains one sentence saying that its own "v1" is a
  scope name, linking the ADR. This is the sentence that stops the next reader
  re-deriving `1.0.0` from a heading — and it is the exact failure shape
  `.claude/rules/milestone-boundary-calls.md` exists for: *"a bounded measurement
  restated as an absolute one hop up the document chain"*, arriving at a version
  number instead of a concurrency figure.

**Verification:** — this is prose and a policy, and the only mechanical part is
cheap. `tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py::test_the_declared_version_is_pre_one_point_zero`
asserts `[project].version` starts `"0."`, with the ADR's filename in the
assertion message so a future bump to `1.0.0` fails pointing at the argument it
has to overturn. That is the whole of what a test can honestly say here; **do
not** add a case grepping README for the word "Beta", which is a
change-detector on a sentence this task exists to make readable. The prose half
is verified by the PRD link check in `.claude/rules/prd-maintenance.md` —
scoped to `docs/prd/` plus `CLAUDE.md` and `README.md`, which is exactly the
three files this task touches — printing `OK`.

**Acceptance:**
- `ls docs/prd/decisions/` shows the new ADR; `README.md` and
  `docs/prd/09-roadmap.md` both link it; the PRD link check prints `OK`.
- `README:12` reads "Beta." and the sentence after it names `0.x` and links the
  ADR.
- The ADR's evidence section carries all three counts with the command that
  produced each, not the adjectives. A version argument resting on "it feels
  early" is the one a later reader overturns in an afternoon.
- The ADR states what `1.0.0` would *cost*, not only what blocks it: under
  semver a `1.x` line owes a deprecation path for every wire field, and PRD 07's
  own answer today is *"breaking changes get `/v2`"* (`:962`), which is a
  different mechanism from semver majors and would have to be reconciled.

### Task R4 — `CHANGELOG.md`, and the first entry is ten milestones rather than a placeholder

**Depends on:** R3   **Files:** `CHANGELOG.md` (new), `tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py`

Keep a Changelog 1.1.0 (<https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/>) — chosen over
Conventional-Commits generation, and the reason is this repository specifically.
The commit subjects here are already prose written for a reader
(`fix(fixtures): the committed data was real, and the note said it was not`), so
a generator would produce 678 lines of something already better said in
`docs/plans/`; and 1.1.0's six categories — Added, Changed, Deprecated, Removed,
Fixed, Security — are the categories a stranger scans. The format's
`[Unreleased]` section is what M11 writes into, which is the property that keeps
this file from being written once and abandoned.

**The first entry is `## [0.1.0] - <the tag date>` and it covers M1–M10 as one
release, because it is one.** There are no tags today (`git tag -l` is empty,
verified 2026-08-13), so there is no earlier release to diff against. Material,
in order of usefulness:

- `docs/plans/progress.md:15–24` — the milestone table, each row with its merge
  commit and its test count: M1 `addb28d` (2026-07-30), M2 `0192bf6` (2026-07-30),
  M3 `970d2b6` (2026-07-31), M4 `1b37799` (2026-08-01), M5 `66e0b64` (2026-08-02),
  M6 `b0c04e5` (2026-08-03), M7 `6d9b2a1` (2026-08-05), M8 `1b54ffd` (2026-08-10),
  M9 `029092c` (2026-08-12).
- `CLAUDE.md`'s own milestone table, which already says in one line each what
  every milestone delivers and what it was live-verified against. That table is
  the right length for a changelog entry and the wrong voice — it addresses a
  contributor, and this addresses a user.
- `README:10–97`, for what the surface is.

**Three things the entry must carry that a generated one would not.**

- **Under `Security`:** the playback-token grant (ADR-0012 accepted, ADR-0029
  narrowed the artifact and not the grant) and **no authentication on any
  route**. A changelog whose `Security` heading is absent reads as "nothing to
  say here" (`README:528`'s own argument about zeros, one artefact over).
- **Under `Fixed`:** `1196a9d` and `969dcbf`, the two commits that removed real
  IMDb rows from the working tree, linked to R12's PRD 04 paragraph. A first
  release that hides its own licensing repair in the git log is exactly the
  shape the repair was about.
- **Two measured refusals, because they are the project's most transferable
  results and no category holds them naturally**: ADR-0002's typo-tolerance gate
  ran and **failed** (`README:20–26`), and query expansion measured **worse**
  (MRR 0.733 → 0.373, `README:398–408`) and ships off by default. Put them under
  `Added` beside the features they qualify, not in a footnote.

**Failing test first:** `tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py::test_the_changelog_names_the_version_that_ships`
— parses `CHANGELOG.md` for the first `## [<version>]` heading and asserts it
equals `importlib.metadata.version("usher")`. At HEAD there is no `CHANGELOG.md`
and the case fails on `FileNotFoundError`. Its positive control is that the
heading scan found **at least one** version heading before the comparison — a
regex that matched nothing and a changelog whose top entry is right are
otherwise the same green. `[Unreleased]` is skipped by the scan and the case
says so in its docstring, which is the one design decision in it: the check is
*"the newest released version is the one that ships"*, not *"the newest heading
is"*.

**Acceptance:**
- `CHANGELOG.md` exists, carries a Keep-a-Changelog 1.1.0 header linking the
  format and the semver policy from R3, an `[Unreleased]` section, and one
  `## [0.1.0]` entry.
- The entry names all ten milestones and, for each, what a *user* gets — not
  what a task delivered. A line that only makes sense with `docs/plans/` open
  has failed.
- Every claim in it is either already in the PRD/README with a citation, or is a
  number this task re-measured. **No number is transcribed from a commit message
  without checking it** — `.claude/rules/mutation-sweeps.md` records two cases of
  a count in a commit body disagreeing with the suite.
- The `[0.1.0]` link target is the compare URL
  `https://github.com/anirudhlath/usher/releases/tag/v0.1.0`, which does not
  resolve until R13 pushes the tag. That is correct and is stated in R13's
  acceptance, not silently left broken.

### Task R5 — `CONTRIBUTING.md`, and a pull-request template that renders the gate rather than restating it

**Depends on:** R3   **Files:** `CONTRIBUTING.md` (new), `.github/pull_request_template.md` (new), `tests/unit/test_contributing.py` (new)

`.github/` holds exactly one file today (`find .github -type f` →
`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, 46 lines). There is no `CONTRIBUTING.md`, no PR
template, and no issue template.

The content a stranger needs is already written and scattered: `CLAUDE.md`'s
**Commands** section has the gate, the test selections, the database commands
and the CLI; `CLAUDE.md`'s **Conventions that will bite you** has the eight rules
that get a PR sent back; `.claude/rules/` has the evidence. **`CONTRIBUTING.md`
does not restate any of it — it points at it and adds the three things a
contributor needs that a maintainer's file does not have:** how to get a
database up (`docker compose up -d postgres`, then `uv run alembic upgrade head`),
that `tests/integration/` needs Docker and starts its own Postgres through
testcontainers (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:40–46`'s comment says why there is no
`services:` block), and that **TDD is not a preference here** — `CLAUDE.md`
requires a failing test first, and every plan in `docs/plans/` names the case
before the code.

**The one thing worth writing out rather than linking is the mutation-sweep
rule**, because it is the convention most likely to bite a second contributor:
*"a mutation sweep mutates the working tree in place, so nothing else may use
that tree while it runs"* (`CLAUDE.md`). A contributor who runs the suite while
a maintainer sweeps has invalidated the sweep and neither will know.

**The PR template is a rendering of the gate, not a copy of it.** Five
unchecked lines — `ruff check .`, `ruff format --check .`, `mypy src tests`,
`lint-imports`, `pytest` — plus two questions this repository's review rounds
keep asking: *"which test fails without this change, and was it seen red?"* and
*"which claim in `docs/prd/` does this invalidate?"* (`CLAUDE.md`'s **Keep the
PRD current**: *"Update the PRD in the same commit as the change that
invalidates it"*). Markdown checkboxes are correct **in the template**, which is
a form a human fills in; they are the thing this plan format forbids in a
*plan*, and the distinction is worth one sentence in the file so nobody
"fixes" it.

**Failing test first:** `tests/unit/test_contributing.py::test_the_contributing_gate_is_the_gate_claude_md_states`
— parses the fenced `bash` block under `CLAUDE.md`'s `### The gate` heading and
the corresponding block in `CONTRIBUTING.md`, strips trailing `#` comments from
each line, and asserts the two command lists are equal as ordered lists. At HEAD
it fails on `CONTRIBUTING.md` not existing. **Stripping the comments is
load-bearing:** `CLAUDE.md`'s gate line carries
`# architecture contracts — 10 kept, 0 broken`, and comparing raw lines would
make this a contract-count detector that goes red on an unrelated eleventh
contract. Its positive control is `assert len(commands) == 5` on the `CLAUDE.md`
side before the comparison — a fence parser that found nothing compares two empty
lists and passes.

**Acceptance:**
- `CONTRIBUTING.md` exists and links `CLAUDE.md`, `docs/prd/README.md` and
  `SECURITY.md`; a stranger can get from clone to a green `uv run pytest` using
  only it and R13's quickstart.
- `.github/pull_request_template.md` exists and GitHub renders it on a new PR
  (verified by opening the compare view, not by reading the file).
- The gate list in `CONTRIBUTING.md` is the five commands, in `CLAUDE.md`'s
  order, and the new test proves it. **The contract count is not restated in
  `CONTRIBUTING.md`** — it lives in exactly one place, the trailing comment on
  `CLAUDE.md:222` (`# architecture contracts — 10 kept, 0 broken`), it has been
  wrong there before (M9's A1 corrected it from 8 to 9), and a second copy is a
  second chance to be wrong.
- `CONTRIBUTING.md` states the single-maintainer reality (R6's word for it) so a
  contributor knows what response time to expect. A contributing guide that
  implies a team is a promise nobody made.

### Task R6 — `SECURITY.md`, and a supported-version table one person can actually keep

**Depends on:** R3, R4   **Files:** `SECURITY.md` (new), `tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py`

None exists. GitHub surfaces `SECURITY.md` on the repository's Security tab and
in the "Report a vulnerability" flow, so this file is the one community file a
stranger reaches *without being told about it*.

**Two things in it must be real, and boilerplate is worse than absence in both.**

**The contact.** The maintainer is one person, `anirudhlath` (`git remote -v` →
`https://github.com/anirudhlath/usher.git`; `LICENSE` → `Copyright (c) 2026
Anirudh Lath`). The task's decision is *which* channel, and there are two honest
options: GitHub's **private vulnerability reporting** (repository Settings →
Security → enable; it creates a private advisory thread and needs no address
published), or a mailto. **Prefer private vulnerability reporting**, because it
costs the maintainer no published address, gives the reporter a tracked thread,
and — the part that matters here — its absence is *visible*: if it is not
enabled, the "Report a vulnerability" button is simply not there, so the file
cannot claim a channel that does not work. **Enabling it is part of this task,
not an assumption**, and the acceptance names the check. A mailto in the file
with no enabled reporting is exactly the boilerplate this task exists to avoid.

**The supported-version statement.** The temptation is a table with an LTS
column. A single maintainer with no release history cannot keep one. The honest
statement, and it is short: **only the most recent release is supported; there
are no backports; `0.x` means a fix may arrive in a minor version rather than a
patch.** That is a promise one person keeps by doing nothing unusual, and it
follows from R3's `0.x` rather than being a second, unrelated policy.

**Three things the file must name because a reader will otherwise find them the
hard way**, each already documented and none of them a defect:

- **No route in this API requires authentication** (R10's subject) — including
  every `/admin` route.
- **A `direct` playback target's URL carries the source's session token**
  (`08-operations.md:244`, ADR-0012), and the M9 ticket made the *artifact*
  opaque, not the grant narrower (R11's subject).
- **`USHER_SECRET_KEY` is the whole of the credential encryption**, and rotating
  it invalidates every stored source credential until the next write
  (`08-operations.md:232–241`) and every outstanding playback ticket
  (ADR-0029's *"rotate `USHER_SECRET_KEY`, which invalidates every outstanding
  ticket at once"* — the coarse revocation that does exist). Group K's rotation
  command is what makes that a procedure rather than an outage; link the runbook
  Phase 2 writes.

**Failing test first:** `tests/unit/test_release_metadata.py::test_the_security_policy_supports_the_version_that_ships`
— parses `SECURITY.md`'s supported-version table and asserts the supported entry
names `importlib.metadata.version("usher")`'s minor series (`0.1.x` for `0.1.0`).
At HEAD it fails on the missing file. Its positive control is that the table
parse produced at least one row. **This is the assertion that keeps the table
from going stale**, which is the specific way a `SECURITY.md` becomes a lie: the
policy is correct on the day it is written and wrong on the next release, and
nothing re-reads it. Same shape as T5's `AKAS_NAME_MAX_CHARS` binding assertion —
*a claim whose whole purpose is that it agrees with something else needs an
assertion on the agreement*.

**Acceptance:**
- `SECURITY.md` exists at the repository root and GitHub's Security tab renders
  it — checked by loading the tab, not by the file existing.
- Private vulnerability reporting is **enabled** and the "Report a vulnerability"
  button is present; verified with
  `gh api repos/anirudhlath/usher --jq '.security_and_analysis'` **and** by
  loading the page, because an API field that is absent and a feature that is off
  read the same.
- The supported-version statement is one sentence and promises nothing that
  needs a second person.
- The three known-and-accepted items above are named with their ADR or PRD
  citation, so a reporter can tell a documented posture from a finding before
  spending their time on it.

### Task R7 — issue templates, and a chooser that sends the two kinds of report to different questions

**Depends on:** R5   **Files:** `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/bug_report.yml` (new), `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/feature_request.yml` (new), `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/config.yml` (new), `tests/unit/test_issue_templates.py` (new)

`.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/` does not exist. The seventeen issues open today
(`gh issue list --state open`, 2026-08-13) were written by the maintainer against
a measured finding, which is why every one of them names a file, a line and a
number — `#13 "Two per-kind concurrency numbers are bounds, not measurements"`,
`#14 "A covering index … is measured, requested, and declined"`. **A stranger's
issue will not look like that, and the template is the only lever on it.**

**YAML issue forms rather than Markdown templates**, because a form's fields are
*required* and a Markdown template's headings are deleted. The three fields that
matter, and each earns its place from a real diagnosis in this repository:

- **Version**, required — `usher --version` from R1, which is why R1 comes
  first and why this is not the version field every template has by reflex.
- **Which surface** — `usher` CLI, HTTP API, the container, or a bulk import.
  `.claude/rules/config-cli-and-deployment.md` records the CLI and the API
  reaching the same defect through different boundaries with different symptoms;
  knowing which one a reporter used is the first bisect.
- **The command and its full output**, with `--traceback` requested explicitly.
  The CLI's error boundary prints one line by design (`README:186–192`), so a
  bug report will carry that one line and nothing else unless the form asks.

`config.yml` sets `blank_issues_enabled: false` and adds a `contact_links` entry
pointing at `SECURITY.md` — **a vulnerability must not arrive as a public
issue**, and the chooser is the only place a reporter is looking at the moment
they would file one.

**Failing test first:** `tests/unit/test_issue_templates.py::test_every_issue_form_parses_and_asks_for_the_version`
— walks `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/*.yml`, loads each with `yaml.safe_load`
(`pyyaml` is already a dev dependency, `pyproject.toml:67`, and
`tests/unit/test_deployment_config.py` already reads `compose.yml` with it),
asserts each has `name`/`description`/`body`, that every `body` element's `id` is
unique within its file, and that the bug form has a required field whose `id` is
`version`. At HEAD it fails on the directory not existing. Its positive control
is `assert forms, "the template scan found nothing"` plus a named anchor
(`bug_report.yml`) — a glob that matched nothing passes exactly like a glob that
passed, which is this repository's most-repeated finding
(`.claude/rules/fixtures-and-fakes.md`, and the fourth check in
`tests/unit/test_no_third_party_data.py` exists for it).

⚠️ **`config.yml` is deliberately outside the scan's *form* assertions and is
asserted separately** — it has no `body` and would fail the form shape. A scan
that skipped it silently would also skip a real form somebody named `config.yaml`
by mistake, so the exclusion is by exact filename with the reason in the
assertion message, the shape A1's mirror test uses for its two exempt
repositories.

**Acceptance:**
- Opening `https://github.com/anirudhlath/usher/issues/new/choose` shows two
  forms and the security contact link, and offers no blank issue. Verified by
  loading the page.
- Filing a test issue against the bug form and immediately closing it proves the
  required fields are enforced; a form that parses is not a form that renders.
- `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/config.yml`'s contact link resolves to `SECURITY.md`
  on the default branch.
